# Foundation — spec

> The company brain of Form Industries: one shared grid where people, the apps we build, and the agents that work with us meet. This document is the plan every session (human or agent) builds from. **DESIGN.md** is its visual contract. Status is honest: _done_ means it ran and was checked; _partial_ and _stub_ say what's missing.

Last updated 2026-11-27 (the server panel says when the server runs older code than the app, with Restart, §7; a sub-grid put back into its section, the undo of Open as its own grid, and hosted-app sections offer it, §3; pings: a sound, the bell opening the card, Ping in the right-click, who has worked on a grid, §0.25; the updates panel keeps the whole release history, this app's version set large with its latest changes, §0.24; note pages: a note of its own in the side menu's Notes, the same note on any grid, written in blocks, docs/notes.md; the grid behind the grid: the agents' work trail after agenttrail, T3's agents told who else is at work, §0.23; the timeline live and whole, a note and a chat card as one box, §0.22; the timeline of every grid and section, voice and video notes, menus that rise and a chat card without its paper §0.21; a run's trace on the council, the silhouette that adds, CAD cards fed by an app, card copy and paste §0.19; main and the edges branch brought together; one note: the text box and the markdown note are the same card, §3; the council at work, kinds of agent and code mode §0.17; whole-app usability, legibility, spacing & motion pass §0.15; ChatForm second round §0.14; progressive cached images & hosted ChatForm readiness §0.13) · **0.3.0** (`docs/blog/0.3.0.md`; the desktop app updates itself from here on, and from 2026-11-13 every push to `main` publishes the next desktop release by itself) · branch `main` · evidence in `docs/evidence/` (latest: `2026-11-22_usability-pass/`, §0.15) · release notes in `docs/blog/` and Settings → Releases · **the plan is a kanban board**: `docs/plan.json`, GitHub issues, and Settings → Plan in the app (§8)

## 0. How this brief was framed

The original ask (Josh, 2026-11-02) was broad: unify Vision, Creation and the Data Room's UI work into Foundation; rebuild flowform's features with better design and agents; build a company view of BomForm, FactoryForm and the other apps; reach 60 fps; write a spec and a design language; then build, test and push. Before building, five questions were answered:

**1. What would a top professional do that wasn't asked for?**
A principal product engineer would make the board's _data model and protocol_ the product. Every surface (people, the built-in agent, external agents over MCP, embedded apps over postMessage) would read and write one versioned document, so a panel, a wire or an ask is the same object whoever made it. They would carry over the predecessor's hard-won invariants as tests, not as folklore: hashed bearers, archive-never-delete, confirm-to-delete, idempotent agent writes, viewer-write rejection. They would set a performance budget and measure against it rather than promise "60 fps". And they would make the system honest about what isn't wired yet (offline connectors say _offline_ and why).

**2. The rawest material.**
Not summaries of the old app but its source: `flowform-server` (Axum, ~275k lines of Rust: the Yjs room protocol, persistence, auth, search, MCP) and `flowform-client` (GPUI, ~336k lines: LOD, media residency, viewport culling). Vision's `packages/ui` `tokens.css`, `motion.ts` and `gl.ts` are the design truth, ahead of any screenshot. The real HTTP and MCP surfaces of FactoryForm (`docs/API.md`, `server/erp/routes.js`) and BomForm (`lib/bomform/client.ts` in the Data Room). The live services on this machine (Vision Code/T3 on :3783, the Archive on :63727, FactoryForm on :3001).

**3. Methods and tools.**
- **Sync and state:** a CRDT (Yjs) document per board with the y-protocols sync/awareness wire.
- **Storage and search:** SQLite (node:sqlite) for durable state, with an FTS5 index tuned for prefix search on part numbers.
- **Agents and interop:** MCP (streamable HTTP) as the agent interface. The agent runs on two runtimes: Claude Code headless on the person's subscription (`claude -p --output-format stream-json`, the T3-style session) or the Anthropic API on credits. A postMessage bridge carries ports between embedded apps.
- **Rendering:**
  - Off the React hot path: springs write styles directly.
  - Idle-aware WebGL.
  - IntersectionObserver and zoom-gated iframes, which is flowform's iframe-snapshot idea.
  - Memoised layout per state snapshot.
- **Verification:** `node:test` for invariants, a real browser for the UI, and the real services for connectors.

**4. What, other than you or me, shows it's right?**
The running system:
- two browser sessions converge on the same board;
- an agent run through Claude Code changes the board and the change appears live for everyone;
- search finds a STEP file by the part name inside its header;
- FactoryForm's own server answers the connector;
- the tests enforce the invariants;
- screenshots in light and night, on tokens only.

**5. The rewritten brief (what this session executed):**

> Build Foundation v0 as an npm-workspaces monorepo: `apps/server` (Node 22 + TypeScript: SQLite, Yjs board sync, auth, files, search, MCP, agent runtime, connectors, Hermes, REA jobs), `apps/client` (the existing React grid, synced through Yjs, plus panels, the agent layer, ⌘K, the agent dock, login, presentation, design page), `packages/protocol` (the board schema all sides share), and `packages/ui` (Vision's design language as Foundation's tokens). Port flowform's valuable server behaviour and invariants, and drop its Gantt and digital twin. Seed a company board where Vision, BomForm, FactoryForm, the Data Room, Creation and Foundation are live connectors wired by real data flows. Verify in a browser (light and night), run one real agent turn on the subscription runtime, test the invariants, write SPEC.md and DESIGN.md with honest status and the hookups a person must do, and push to `main`.

### 0.1 Huddles and Slack channels (Josh, 2026-11-02)

**The ask.** Right-click should also open a **video portal**: your camera streams live into the board for everyone on it, a spatial huddle. Slack channels become a primitive. You can read and post to a channel, send it links to places on the board, and invite it to a Foundation huddle instead of a Slack one. Everything said is archived with the recordings, like Granola.

**What a professional adds.** A portal costs nothing until someone is in it. People who are only looking watch without sending, and only while the portal is on screen. The call goes with you as you roam the board (a chip in the corner, with the sound). Each browser transcribes **its own** mic, so every line knows its speaker without diarization. Recording is visible to all (REC), and each person records themselves, so nobody's stream is captured by someone else's machine. Posts carry the person's name ("Josh via Foundation"). Links into the board frame the place they point at. Each time people gather is a **session** with its own transcript, recordings and people, searchable from ⌘K and readable by agents.

**Rawest material.** WebRTC's perfect-negotiation pattern. The Web Speech API and MediaRecorder. Slack's Web API: `conversations.*`, `chat.postMessage` with `chat:write.customize` and message metadata. flowform-server's Slack connector (per-user OAuth, read scopes only) and its transcription stub (AWS Transcribe, never wired).

**Methods.** A WebRTC mesh relayed by `/huddle/:board/:huddle`: fine for about six people, with nothing paid or third-party carrying media. STUN, plus TURN when `FOUNDATION_ICE_SERVERS` is set. SQLite for sessions, lines and recording links. Recordings are ordinary uploaded files (archived, never deleted). FTS5 for transcripts. A bot token for Slack, polled while a card is on screen and cached server-side.

**What shows it's right.** Two browsers signed in as two people (`localhost` and `kai.localhost`). Each receives the other's 640×360 video and audio, measured from the `<video>` elements. Typed lines reach the other side live. Both recordings upload and decode. The archive shows the session. ⌘K finds a phrase from it. A Slack card reads, posts, links and invites against a stand-in Slack API. Tests cover the server, sockets included (`apps/server/test/huddle.test.ts`). Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-02_huddles-slack/`.

### 0.2 Today and the Shiba (Josh, 2026-11-11)

**The ask.** A first-run experience before the boards, like the ephemeral Shiba in Vision and HomeForm that you talk to. A dashboard of what to focus on today for the person signed in: links into boards and files that need them, that tag them, or that relate to their job. Their goals, introspected by the Shiba, which answers in natural language while the interface keeps its state. The company's vibe-coded apps (BomForm, FactoryForm, HyperForm …) always in reach. Then a Chrome extension everyone installs as their first stop. Later, the Airform Data Room as a source of people's projects and goals.

**What a professional adds.** One source of truth for the page and the Shiba: the page draws `/api/today` and the Shiba reads the same thing (the `today` tool and a digest in its prompt), so what it says and what you see can't disagree. The Shiba changes the page through tools, not prose: `point_today` lights the items it's talking about, `set_goal` adds and finishes goals, `set_profile` records what you do, and the page refetches when those run. Focus is derived only from what Foundation knows (asks, @mentions, stages owned, files and changes on your boards, your job's words against the search index), never invented, and each item says why it's there. Goals and profiles are personal, not board writes, so a viewer keeps theirs while still unable to change a board. The voice is the person's subscription runtime (Claude Code), not a paid realtime voice: nothing new spends money. Apps are one shared list (`@foundation/protocol` COMPANY_APPS) for the page and the extension. The extension asks for nothing but storage and its own Foundation address, and never holds a credential: the new tab hands off to Foundation's own origin, where the person's sign-in already lives.

**Rawest material.** Vision's `packages/ui/src/shiba.ts` (the WebGL2 shader over Josh's four forms) with `gl.ts`, `motion.ts`, `orb.ts`; ADR-031/032 there; HomeForm's `ShibaDock.tsx` (the full-screen room: words in large light type, history at the side). Foundation's own `asks`, board notes (`noteText`), stage panels, `files`, activity logs and FTS index. Chrome's MV3 `chrome_url_overrides`, `action`, `optional_host_permissions`.

**Methods.** Vendor the shiba (no fork: `packages/ui/src/shiba/README.md` names the Vision commit). A `today.ts` module on the server (profiles and goals tables, focus derivation), four personal tools, a `persona: "shiba"` on the agent (its own system addendum and a `<today>` context instead of the board digest; sessions `ses_shiba…` resume and stay out of the board's session list). A React page with an external store, so tool events drive it. An MV3 extension built from `apps/extension/src` plus Foundation's tokens and font, with icons drawn by the build.

**What shows it's right.** A real Shiba turn on the subscription: asked "What should I tackle first today, and how am I doing on my goals?", it answered from the page's own data and ringed the two items and two goals it named; asked to close one goal and add another, it did both and the page changed with it. Two browsers' worth of checks: `test/today.test.ts` (5: mentions, goal ownership, focus sources and order, the shared digest, a viewer's personal tools) and `e2e/today.spec.ts` (home lands on Today, an ask links to its card, chips, a goal persists, an honest "no runtime" answer, Boards and back). The extension's test checks the package (4); its popup and new tab were run as pages against a signed-in Foundation. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-11_today-shiba/`.

---

### 0.2 Machines in a section: Kiri or Docker (Josh, 2026-11-07)

**The ask.** Start and load virtual machines in a section of Foundation, through Docker or through Kiri (github.com/libracoder/kiri), a lightweight VM host.

**1. What a top professional would do that wasn't asked for.**
- **Not write two drivers.** Kiri is a small Linux VM that serves the **Docker Engine API**, so Kiri and Docker are one wire: the engine's own socket (a named pipe on Windows) and its HTTP API. Shell out to neither CLI, and install nothing.
- **Make "a machine" mean what Kiri means.** A long-lived container named `machine-<name>`, labelled `dev.kiri.machine`, so `kiri machine shell <name>` reaches what Foundation started. Foundation's own labels tie it to its card. The engine stays the truth: the card shows what the engine says, read back while someone looks at it.
- **Treat Start as running third-party code**, because it is. Only a person starts, stops, restarts, removes or loads, from a browser session (a cookie). A bearer token (an agent, MCP or the service token) is refused. Agents may propose a machine and read its state and screen.
- **Remove** destroys the disk, so it needs `confirm`. **Viewers** watch the terminal and can't type in it.
- **Confine by default.** Nothing from this computer is mounted, nothing is privileged, ports publish on 127.0.0.1 only, and memory, CPU and process caps are set.
- **One terminal per machine, shared.** Two people at one shell, like the board itself, with the screen replayed to late joiners and to agents.
- **The machine's ports are the way out.** A published port opens as a page in the section beside it.
- **"Load" means `docker load`.** An image file someone saved goes into the engine from the archive, and the file stays archived.

**2. The rawest material.** Not Kiri's README but its source:
- `crates/kiri/src/machine.rs`: what a machine is: distro images, the sleep loop, labels, `machine-<name>`.
- `crates/kiri/src/daemon/api.rs`: which Engine API routes it serves, its 101 hijack for exec, API 1.47, version-prefix stripping.
- `crates/kiri/src/client.rs` and `docs/features.md`: its sockets: `\\.\pipe\kiri`, `~/.kiri/run/docker.sock`, `/run/kiri/docker.sock`.
- `daemon/hostopts.rs`: it doesn't keep `Memory`/`NanoCpus`; Kiri caps its whole VM instead. *(Wrong for Kiri 0.1.18 on Windows, found running it 2026-11-07: a machine's cgroup has `memory.max` 2 GiB, `cpu.max` 2 CPUs and `pids.max` 1024, the caps Foundation sends.)*
- The Docker Engine API reference: create, start, exec, the hijacked raw stream, resize, load.
- The live engine on this computer (Docker Desktop 28.3, API 1.51, on `\\.\pipe\docker_engine`).

**3. Methods and tools.**
- **Engine:** Node's `http` over the engine's socket path (named pipe or unix socket), with no SDK. Label filters find a card's container. The exec stream is taken over with `Connection: Upgrade` (a 101 response), and the server relays it to browsers over `/machines/:board/:panel/tty`.
- **Terminal:** xterm.js with its fit addon. Its colours come from the tokens through a probe element, so night mixes resolve.
- **State:** the board's Y.Doc (`PanelDoc.machine`) for state everyone sees.
- **Tests:** `node:test` with a stand-in Engine API on a named pipe.
- **Checks in a real browser** with two people, against the real engine.
- **`npm run machines:smoke`:** the driver against whatever engine is installed.

**4. What, other than you or me, shows it's right.**
- **The engine itself.** `docker ps` / `docker inspect` (or `kiri machine list`) shows the container the card claims, with its labels, no mounts, the caps, and a port on 127.0.0.1 only. When it's removed, the engine has nothing left.
- **The machine itself.** `/etc/os-release` typed into the card answers from inside it. A page served on its port opens in the next section, and `curl` gets it.
- **A second person.** Kai's command shows in Ada's terminal.
- **The server's refusals.** A bearer token gets 403; a remove without `confirm` gets 400; a viewer's keys never reach the shell.
- **The tests**, and screenshots day and night on tokens.

**5. The rewritten brief (what this session executed):**

> Add **machines** to Foundation: a section can hold a Linux machine or container that runs on this computer's container engine, **Kiri or Docker**, through the Docker Engine API both serve, over the engine's own socket. A machine is Kiri's kind (`machine-<name>`, `dev.kiri.machine`), labelled with its board and card; the engine is the truth and the card mirrors it. A person picks an image on the card (a distro, one the engine already has, or a `docker save` file loaded from the archive) and presses Start; it pulls with progress, runs confined (no host mounts, not privileged, ports on 127.0.0.1, caps), and opens a terminal shared by everyone on the board (viewers watch). Ports open as pages in the next section. Stop keeps the disk; Remove needs confirm. Agents get `propose_machine` and a read-only `machines` (state, ports, the last screen); every action that runs code needs a person's browser session. Test the invariants against a stand-in engine; check it in a real browser with two people against the real engine on this machine; add a smoke script for Kiri; show it on `#/design` day and night; update SPEC and DESIGN honestly; push to `main`.

Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-07_machines/`.

### 0.3 The crew: agents you can see (Josh, 2026-11-07)

**The ask.** Agent cursors with generated names that help whenever you ask for something. They can take your view and bring you to a section, make primitives, hook up data sources, and move around, improve and organize the interface themselves. You mostly call on them by holding the space bar: a voice input rises from the bottom, and you say what you need.

**1. What a top professional would do that wasn't asked for.**
- **One identity per agent, end to end.** Before this, every agent on a board shared the server's one presence: one ring and no name. Each agent turn now joins the board as its own awareness client, with a generated name and a colour. Every write it makes is signed with that name, in the activity log and as `createdBy` on panels.
- **Anticipation.** An agent's cursor goes to where the change will land and waits a beat (`FOUNDATION_CREW_TRAVEL_MS`, 520 ms) before the change lands. A creation aims at the next free slot in its section's flow. People can follow the work instead of watching cards pop in.
- **Consent over your camera.** Only the person who asked is moved. If they were moving around in the last 1.8 s, the agent asks first (**Go**) instead of yanking the view. **Back** always returns them to where they were.
- **Space still pans.** If a pointer goes down while Space is held, it's a pan, and the sheet stands down before it has heard anything. A quick tap types instead, for no mic or no recogniser.
- **Bounded crews.** Helpers come only inside a person's ask: at most two per ask, never a helper's helper, at most six agents on a board. Every agent can be stopped from the sheet, all of yours at once with **Stop all** (2026-11-07), and no cursor outlives its run. **Follow** (2026-11-07) rides your view along with any working agent until you move, and agents can **point** at a cell or a port (`point_at`).

**2. The rawest material.**
- The y-protocols awareness wire (client ids, clocks, the 30 s timeout).
- Claude Code's `stream-json` events and the MCP calls they make.
- The board's Y.Doc.
- The camera spring.
- The mic itself: Web Speech for words, and an `AnalyserNode` for the real input level.

**3. Methods and tools.**
- **Identity:** one `Awareness` per agent, on its own small Y.Doc, bridged into the board's awareness (it renews every 15 s and leaves cleanly).
- **MCP:** a crew id carried on the MCP URL (`&crew=`) and honoured only for the bearer's own person and board.
- **Motion:** springs that write `transform` directly (travel k 140, c 22; the arrival on the pop spring).
- **Tests:** a `script` runtime (`FOUNDATION_AGENT_SCRIPT=1`, tests only) that runs a fixed playbook through the real tools, so the browser checks are the same every time; Playwright with two people; `node:test` for the invariants.

**4. What, other than you or me, shows it's right.**
- **A second person's browser.** Kai sees each agent by name, and its cursor is already inside the section when each card lands (measured: 0.5–2.5 s ahead).
- **The board's own activity**, which names every agent that wrote.
- **The asker's measured camera.** Ada is moved and **Back** restores her to within 2 px. Kai's camera doesn't move.
- **The server's awareness**, which is empty once the run ends.
- **A real Claude Code turn.** The runtime ran here, but its sign-in had expired (§7), so this check still waits.

**5. The rewritten brief (what this session executed):**

> Add a crew to Foundation. Hold Space and a voice sheet rises from the bottom: a live mic level, your words as heard; release sends, Esc drops, a quick tap types. Each ask gets an agent with a generated name and its own cursor and colour. Several can work at once, and a lead may call in up to two helpers within your ask. A cursor travels to what it's about to change before the change lands, and says what it's doing. Agents create primitives (sections, notes, tables with data, panels), hook up data sources (connectors, apps, wires), and move, rename and organize, all through the one tool surface, with every write attributed by name. An agent can bring the asker to a section: it glides there, or asks if they're busy, and Back returns them; others just watch the agent go. Prove it with a scripted runtime in two-browser e2e, server tests for identity, caps, attribution, viewer refusal and cleanup, one real subscription turn, and `#/design` day and night. Update SPEC and DESIGN, then push to main.

Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-07_crew/`.

### 0.4 The council and the Character studio (Josh, 2026-11-14)

**The ask.** An avatar system for the council of agents on any section or grid: seen from the chest up, head to toe in some circumstances; press the council on a section or the whole grid and they come up on top, one per agent at work there, colour-coded to the cursors they send around the board. Sets of characters to pick from, made from the folders in `C:\Design\CharacterReference` by the WildChild pipeline (inspiration → character sheet → Meshy 3D → skeleton → animation, with a state machine on their emotional responses); the anime set animated with mesh-avatar-studio. And the pipeline itself in Foundation: a board that holds the inspiration, the sheets and the 3D models, where more can be made by using it.

**1. What a top professional would do that wasn't asked for.**
- **Make the character a card, and the pipeline its state.** A character is a card (`panel: "character"`) whose stages the server keeps in the board's `characters` map, so everyone on the board watches a sheet arrive, and a person can't forge "ready" over the socket.
- **Stage it, and say the price before the press.** Every stage spends Meshy credits, so each is its own button with its cost on it, pressed by a person at a browser (`requirePerson`: an agent's bearer gets 403). Agents may add a character and describe it (`add_character`). WildChild's rule, kept: look at each result before paying for the next.
- **Never send a charged request twice.** The request is written down before it's sent; a send with no answer (a dropped connection, the server stopping mid-send) is "may have been charged" and won't go again until the person says so. A refusal from Meshy (402, 429) charges nothing and can simply be pressed again.
- **Keep the lineage.** Each task keeps its prompt word for word, the model, who pressed it and the SHA-256 of every reference it was drawn from (never their bytes): the Vision Archive's rule that a generated image is a proposal with its request attached.
- **One renderer for the whole council.** Six seats are one WebGL canvas with a scissor per seat, not six renderers (Vision's Motion Lab note). On the studio board only the card under the pointer plays; the rest hold a still.
- **Measure the clips instead of trusting their names.** Meshy's plain "Idle" swings the torso through 150°, "Victory Cheer" spins, "Checkout Gesture" turns away: wrong for a figure that faces the room. Sixteen candidate clips were generated on a real skeleton and their shoulder yaw measured; the moods use the ones that stay square to the camera.
- **A mood is a state machine, not a label.** Held moods (idle, thinking, working, talking) follow what the agent's cursor says; beats (waving, happy, sad, surprised) come from changes of status, play out, and settle back.
- **The same agent wears the same character everywhere**: a steady pick by name, shared when there aren't enough, and overridable by anyone (stored server-side, by agent name).

**2. The rawest material.**
- WildChild's pipeline as code: `tools/meshy_task.mjs` (write-before-send, hashes, Meshy-only downloads), `tools/meshy_hunter_v8_pipeline.mjs` (the A-pose view rules, front-then-turnarounds, `multi-image-to-3d` → `rigging` → `animations`), and its lessons (`docs/lookdev/2026-10-24_hunter-child-v8/README.md`): no pose conversion on stylised bodies, use the clips' GLB not the bare rigged one, strip the hips' travel.
- The Vision Archive's contracts (`packages/schema/src/archive.ts`, `docs/prompts/ASTRA_IMAGE_BRIEF.md`): prompt verbatim, provider and model on every image, references as hashes, a proposal is not a decision.
- Meshy's own reference (docs.meshy.ai, read 2026-11-14): `image-to-image` (nano-banana-pro, 9 credits), `multi-image-to-3d` (30), `rigging` (5; bipeds only, face to +Z), `animations` (3 a clip, `action_ids` returns one file with a clip per action), `GET /animations/library` (678 clips), `GET /balance`.
- mesh-avatar-studio's source and agent guide (`docs/agent-guide.md`, `packages/runtime`): the rig is coordinates read off zoomed grids, checked on overlays and 64 rendered poses; the runtime (`mesh-avatar` 0.8.0) takes a `.mavatar` and exposes `setEmotion`, `setExpression`, `setSpeaking`.
- The crew's awareness (`crew.ts` `CrewState`): `hue`, `status`, `doing`, `target`, `guide`. That is everything a seat and its mood are made of.
- The 30 pictures in `C:\Design\CharacterReference`, looked at.

**3. Methods and tools.**
- **Server:** `meshy.ts` (a thin client; `MESHY_API_BASE` lets a test stand in), `characters.ts` (SQLite `characters`, `character_tasks`, `character_boards`, `crew_avatars`; the stage planner and prompts; a poll loop per task, resumed at boot; results into the archive with `storeFile`).
- **Protocol:** `packages/protocol/src/characters.ts`: `CharacterState`, the stages of each form, `MOODS`, `MOOD_CLIPS`, `MOOD_FACES`, the mood machine (`baseMood`, `nextMood`) and seating (`seatCrew`), pure and tested from the server's tests.
- **Client:** three.js (`GLTFLoader`, `AnimationMixer` with cross-fades, a scissor per seat) for 3D; `mesh-avatar` for the 2D mesh; the council as a DOM overlay that takes the pointer only on its bar.
- **Tests:** `node:test` with a stand-in Meshy on a port; Playwright with a stand-in Meshy that serves a real skinned GLB with a clip per action, and the script runtime for the agents.
- **The real thing:** the pipeline run through the server's own routes with the real key, as a signed-in person, stage by stage, looking at every image.

**4. What, other than you or me, shows it's right.**
- **Meshy's own answers.** Six characters went sheet → views → model → skeleton → clips on the real API; each clips file is one GLB with 8 named animations, a skin of 24 joints and its texture (read back from the file).
- **The board.** The Character studio board holds the 30 inspiration pictures, 11 character cards, and every sheet, view, model and clip as files in the archive.
- **A browser.** The council draws all six 3D characters and all five mesh avatars live (frames counted on the one canvas), by day and at night; a scripted agent takes a seat whose `--crew` is its cursor's.
- **The server's refusals.** A bearer press gets 403 and nothing reaches Meshy; a forged `characters` entry sent over a socket is put back.
- **The numbers.** Shoulder yaw per clip, measured in a browser on Mochi's skeleton, decided the mood clips. Credits: 2,358 before, 1,680 after the 3D set (678 spent, 48 of them on the clip measurements), then 18 more on Sora's portrait, twice. Every task on the account was read back from Meshy's own lists to account for them: the 271 credits that went between 19:14 and 19:22 were another session's (three sheets, ten views, three models, two rigs, eighteen clips), not this work's.

**5. The rewritten brief (what this session executed):**

> Give Foundation's agents characters, and a studio that makes them. **The council**: ☺ on the top bar, a section's tools, its menu and ⌘K raises a bar at the foot of the window with a seat for each agent at work on the board or in that section: its character from the chest up on its cursor's colour, what its cursor says above it, its name below; a mood from a state machine over its awareness; pressed, it stands head to toe with what it was asked, Follow, Stop and the characters it can appear as. With nobody at work, the bench of characters and a place to ask. **The Character studio**: a board (⌘K) with a column per set (Anime, Animals, Abstract): inspiration above, characters below. A character card draws its sheet from the pictures picked and the words given, then its views, then a model, a skeleton and a clip per mood (3D), or a portrait and a `.mavatar` (mesh avatar), one priced press at a time, a person's only; the state is the server's, the results are archived files with their lineage. Run it for real on the three reference folders, measure the clips, rig the anime set with mesh-avatar-studio, test the invariants against a stand-in, check it in a browser day and night, write SPEC and DESIGN honestly.

Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-14_council-characters/`. How to make more: `docs/characters.md`.

### 0.5 One interface (Josh, 2026-11-15)

**The ask.** The server connection in the top bar. The start experience (Today) hosted in the Foundation interface as a special kind of board: the Shiba, voice, and on the right a Todos panel drawn in white over the background with the top vibe-coded apps in it; Today above the grids in the side menu, and the side menu open on a first run. Release and plan as sub-tabs of Settings, out of the side menu; Settings opening on the actual settings (colour scheme, defaults, computer-use permissions), not the plan, with the side menu still there. The design language there too, cleaned up and presented as a board; the plan as a board with a new kanban primitive in one large section. No Upload / New section / Fit in the menu. Agent connections as a Settings tab, and the avatars made for the council, assignable to the connected agents. Folders become **All Grids**; a grid and a board are the same thing and the word is grid; no "new project folder": grids of grids, by dragging a grid into another, which makes it a child in the side menu and links it on the parent grid. No agent-layer button: connections show on hovering inputs and outputs, with what is going out of one primitive into another. No agent button on cards (agents work at the section level); a card that holds an agent shows it as a pill with its picture. The council belongs to the agents activated for a section, with an icon of an actual council, on top of the section; talk to all of them or to one; a button for a pane on the right with the agent's service and whether it's local or cloud; a silhouette as they materialise, preloaded; the grid's council as a panel from the top that moves to the centre when the right pane opens; add agents from it; a small mark of who each is (Claude, ChatGPT, Kimi, DeepSeek). The 3D animal avatars deleted and remade as mesh avatars from the animal photos; every character card rendering itself, falling back to its last picture under load, and no empty image links.

**What a professional adds.** One shell, three places (`lib/place.ts`): the address says which, so links, back and reload work, and nothing remounts the top bar or the side menu. Settings that are really the server's are the server's (`prefs.ts`, per person; a setting only ever narrows what agents may do), and the device's stay on the device. The plan and the design language are real grids the server lays out once (`systemgrids.ts`), not pages dressed as grids: they pan, zoom, take notes beside them and sync like any other. The parent link of a grid of grids is the server's (`boards.ts`: a loop refused, a section that stands for the child made once and renamed with it), so two people filing the same grid can't each make one. Who is on a council is the server's too (`posted.ts`, a server-only map on the grid): assigning starts nothing and spends nothing, and what an agent is (its service, cloud or local) is read from the server's own knowledge of it, never from the request. A crew member says which agent it is (`via`), so a working cursor takes its assigned seat instead of a second one. The vendors' logos stay out of the chrome: monograms in the ink. A still is cut from its flat ground in the browser, so a silhouette is a figure and not a box. Live characters are a budget that follows the frames, not a promise.

**What shows it's right.** `apps/server/test/grids.test.ts` (11): the parent link and its section, loops, the forged-council refusal over a socket, settings' known values, the system grids laid out once. `apps/client/e2e/one-interface.spec.ts` (5) and the rewritten `today`, `council` and `section-agents` specs (13): a first run lands on Today with the menu open and the panel's type white; Settings opens on General and a choice takes effect on the page and on the server; Plan is one kanban card on a grid and Design language twelve chapter cards; a grid dragged onto another is its child in the menu and a section on the parent, and comes back; the council drops under the top bar in the middle of the open paper and moves over when the agent's pane opens, stands over its section otherwise, seats only the assigned and the working; a port's label names where its value goes and its wire lights. The four animal avatars were looked at pose by pose (eyes closed, mouth open, ±30°) before they were brought in. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-15_one-interface/`.


### 0.6 Today in white, the council as a band, adding agents, the studio's pipeline (Josh, 2026-11-16)

**The ask.** Today's row in the side menu as a pill with a notification mark and a tiny Shiba; Today itself all Shiba, drawn in white, a glass composer, larger type, no New conversation; the Shiba's conversation able to show images and plans and to fold code and commands into things the right panel opens; a small T3 on the right that expands to the whole. The council as a full panel that pushes the interface down (under the top bar for a grid, at the top of a section for a section) with the agents cut off along it. A real flow for adding an agent, in the agents panel: local models, Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini, Kimi, DeepSeek, an avatar (a default abstract one if none is picked), a T3 session named for the agent, and signing in through a built-in Kiri VM and browser shown as Foundation's own browser tool, with the agent panel built like Claude Code's (changes, browser, files, terminal, side chat, background tasks, plan, keep awake). The avatars made again through the creative pipeline, shown as a flow of nodes between inspiration and output on the Character studio, with approve and archive deciding what the avatar picker offers. Cards: let a character be removed, the hover bar push content down, Ctrl+scroll through a card, a 2 px scroll bar, resize handles as a gleam on hover. New avatars: Sage torso-up, a white and a black shiba with fox-like faces after Mochi, the abstract characters as mesh avatars, some that are only shapes.

**What was built, and what was not.** Built: everything about Today, the band, the add flow (who, connect, avatar, add) with its default avatar and a named session for one of this computer's agents, the Background face of the agents panel, the detail pane, the pipeline row with its flow card and approval, the card fixes (`docs/evidence/2026-11-16_card-chrome/`), and the avatars (ten more mesh avatars: Sage from the torso up, the fox-faced shibas Shiro and Kuro, the abstract four, and Dot, Quad and Tri; 171 credits). **Not built** (said here rather than implied): signing an agent in through a Kiri VM with a browser shown as a browser tool (signing in is still done in a terminal on the computer, and the flow says so); the agent panel as Claude Code's set of panes (there are three faces: at work, add, background; no changes, files, terminal or browser panes for an agent's session); T3 "mini" is a list of its sessions with Open and Stop and a button that opens T3 whole in a tab, not T3's own interface redrawn on the grid; images, plans and folds are drawn in the Shiba's conversation on Today, not yet in the session column.

**What shows it's right.** `apps/server/test/studio-pipeline.test.ts` (3), `apps/client/test/today-talk.test.ts` (3), `e2e/council.spec.ts` (8: the band's place and the grid's inset, the add flow to a seated agent, approval before a character can be worn, the section's band inside its section), `e2e/section-agents.spec.ts`, `e2e/today.spec.ts`. The avatars were looked at pose by pose before they were brought in. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-16_today-council-agents/`.


### 0.7 What a press grabs, sub-grids, the council on its section's edge, a guided agent setup, the character sheet (Josh, 2026-11-17)

**The ask.** A left press that grabs only the paper, a section's top handle or a menu ("part of the reason I can't delete things, is because left mouse is always grabs"); Ctrl always zooming and Alt+scroll scrolling a card, with a small hint on roll-over that can be dismissed. Character cards better organised typographically, with a larger, more WYSIWYG editor that is part of the card, and lines between the sections. *Open as its own grid* making a real sub-grid that can be pulled out and moved to other grids, with a preview and a link where it was. The side menu, the tabs and search redesigned. The right-click menu with Add and Live side by side and the grid's own actions as a row of icons with roll-over names. Add a desktop fixed in Kiri, and the in-use ring kept inside the section. The council as figures and silhouettes rising from the section's top, not a solid panel, with no text box: talk on roll-over, buttons at the section's top right. An agent setup that holds your hand, with three ways for Claude (an API key, signing in with a Claude account as Vision does through T3, mounting Claude through the inbuilt terminal), applied to every agent. Status icons above the agents' heads. The animal characters given robust expression sheets, several emotions and poses, before they go to mesh-avatar-studio, and the flow of generation as nodes in the middle of the Character studio with a Generate button that adds and arranges. The frowny face in a section's corner. Today's room behind every panel.

**What was built, and what was not.** Built, in five slices. *The pointer*: a left drag moves something only from the paper, a section's header or a card's bar (an image or a video by a grip in its corner); ⌘/Ctrl-wheel always zooms and Alt-wheel scrolls the card under the pointer, with a mark that says so until it's dismissed or learnt; a section's menu in three bands with arrow keys. *Sub-grids*: ⤢ on a section asks once and the server makes a grid inside this one from it in one move (written to the new grid before anything is taken off the old, ids kept so search, links and logs still point at the cards, what can't move named and left, membership copied); the section keeps a preview, and when the grid is moved away the old grid keeps the card and says where it went. *Machines*: a press the server refuses is said on the card (a server older than the app is named as that, which is what "doesn't work" was), an install can be stopped, and the in-use ring is the terminal well's or the screen's, 2 px at any zoom, the only ring while it shows. *The Character studio*: the card as a sheet with *Who it is* as formatted text on the card, an `expressions` stage for both forms, and a set's pipeline as nine node cards joined by the grid's wires with Generate, ＋ New and a finished card placed by the server. *The council, setup and chrome*: the council is no band or panel; agents stand on their section's top edge beside silhouettes that are places to fill, with marks above each head, a line on roll-over and three buttons at the top right; the agents panel has a Council tab; Connect is guided ways with numbered steps (three for Claude); the side menu, the tabs and the search pane are redrawn; Today's room is the whole window; a card's ✕ keeps a 26 px press at any size; the council's mark is three filled figures (the outlined one read as a frowning face at 13 px: that was the frowny face). **Not built, or not verified** (said here rather than implied): *mounting Claude through the inbuilt terminal* is not built: Foundation has no terminal on the computer it runs on (a machine card's terminal is a separate computer and can't sign the host in), so the third way tells the person to use the computer's own terminal; links to key pages open in the system browser, not one inside Foundation; the Claude API-key way was never run with a real key, and whether T3's Claude driver takes `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` this way is unverified; the account way was built from Foundation's own T3 host and Vision's implementation was not read; the Council tab shows what you said and each agent's live line, not the agents' replies, and what was said is kept for that window only; voice in the council's line was not exercised in a browser test; an agent is judged offline from the agents list only; a council standing over its neighbour can cover that neighbour's lower cards while it's open (meant). No expression sheet was generated for any character, the animals included (9 credits each, 54 for the six animals: a person's press), the stage has only run against the stand-in Meshy, and mesh-avatar-studio does not yet use the sheet's panels; the studio's upgrade has not been run on the live studio. The grab rule is the web renderer's only (Rune's own notes and images still grab from anywhere, and Rune's e2e was not run). Hosted-app sections have no ⤢ that makes a grid, and there is no undo of one. The desktop was added in the packaged app's transport, not in its window; Fedora, Rocky, Arch and openSUSE still can't have one. The server on :4410 was not restarted, so the running app serves none of this yet (top of this file; §7).

**What shows it's right.** `e2e/pointer.spec.ts` (7), `test/card-scroll.test.ts` (9), `test/menu.test.ts` (8); `apps/server/test/subgrids.test.ts` (8), `e2e/sub-grid.spec.ts` (5); `apps/server/test/machines-desktop.test.ts` (15), `test/machine-words.test.ts` (3), `e2e/machines-fixes.spec.ts` (4), the desktop smoke on Kiri 0.1.18 with Ubuntu, Debian and Alpine; `apps/server/test/characters.test.ts` (15), `studio-pipeline.test.ts` (4), `e2e/character-studio.spec.ts` (9); `e2e/council.spec.ts` (8), `e2e/section-agents.spec.ts` (5), `e2e/today.spec.ts`, `e2e/one-interface.spec.ts`, on ports above 15000, with typecheck, and day and night looked at. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-17_pointer/`, `2026-11-17_sub-grids/`, `2026-11-17_machines-fixes/`, `2026-11-17_character-studio/` (each with a `NOTES.md` saying how each part was checked) and `2026-11-17_council-setup-chrome/` (14 shots).


### 0.8 Collections: the image pipeline as primitives (Josh, 2026-11-17)

**The ask.** "Sections and primitives to capture the process we use for generating images and piping them into the mesh avatar studio system, and a primitive for that, that can output images or character cards like we have stored in Character studio into a collection, that can be piped into a section that automatically lays out the products of that into the section. The collector should be able to also be expanded to be able to drag out copies of those cards onto the grid." Fable's 2026-11-17 round had already made the studio's flow nine wired node cards (§0.7); a collector, a section it lays out into and copies dragged out of it had not been built.

**What was built, and what was not.** Built: a **collection** card (`panel: "collection"`, ❖) with an input and an output. The server gathers into it what the cards wired into it make (`apps/server/src/collections.ts`): a stage node's wire brings the pictures drawn at that stage (Sheet the sheets, Expressions the expression sheets, Views the portrait or the back and the side by the port the wire leaves, Mesh avatar and Moods the stills, Inspiration its pictures), the Output node's new output brings the finished characters, another collection what it holds; each once, kept until a person takes it out. **Lay out in** (or its output dragged onto a section) pipes it into a section: everything is laid there as a docked card the section's flow arranges, a picture as an image card at its own shape, a character as a **copy of its card**, and what comes in later follows. **Expand** opens it to a grid of tiles; a tile pulled out onto a section is a copy there where it's let go (kept inside the section); onto an empty cell or past an edge, a new section is made for it. A character's copy is its own card and row, marked `copyOf`: it shows the character, is never offered as an avatar twice, and has nothing to press. **Character pipeline**, in a section's right-click menu, lays the whole flow into that section with a collection after Output, on any grid (the section becomes `Pipeline · <Set>`). Agents have the same as tools. A press on a port a wire lands on is now the port's (an already-wired output can start another wire), which wiring a stage on into a collection needed. **Not built**: Rune doesn't draw a collection (the web renderer only); no wire is drawn from a collection to the section it lays out into (the card says where and links there); nothing reaches mesh-avatar-studio itself: the Mesh avatar node still takes a `.mavatar` brought in by hand (§7).

**What shows it's right.** `apps/server/test/collections.test.ts` (6), `apps/client/e2e/collections.spec.ts` (6), `e2e/pointer.spec.ts` (7, the menu's list updated), and `e2e/character-studio.spec.ts`, `council.spec.ts`, `card-chrome.spec.ts`, `section-agents.spec.ts`, `sub-grid.spec.ts` passing unchanged, on ports above 15000; day and night looked at. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-17_collections/` (`NOTES.md`).

### 0.9 Themes, a real browser, a release page, sheets, and a card's menu (Josh, 2026-11-18)

**The ask.** "The Applications i.e. browser doesn't seem to work, please implement the same in-built browser that claude has, and make it usable and readable by agents. The terminal is mostly there, just needs to maybe return values without so many new lines." Changes "more designed like a release page, perhaps some images, diagrams or icons, a small description below", white on Today and normal elsewhere, through "a theme-ing system … the today theme is what is applied during the today mode, but the rest of the themes are applied depending on the properties of the Grid or application that is in focus" ("returning to a normal grid should have changed the terminal text to a readable color, however it stayed white"). T3's pane themed, its expand opening "the app fully in Foundation as the T3 APP as a tab … accessible in the grids panel with an appropriate icon", as most vibe-coded apps should. The wheel zooming by default; the day/night orb right of the profile; the section adjuster running the length of its section's edge and no further; right-click on primitives ("removing an item, minimizing it, showing its inputs and outputs in greater detail"); and a new section starting with "a few full-screen primitives starting with tables", the table spanning the section and drawn "on the grid itself", unpinnable, with primitives able to "span, pin, unpin themselves … and create collections as well".

**What was built, and what was not.** Built, in six slices. *Themes* (DESIGN §2.1): the shell sets one theme for the place in focus (`paper`, a grid's own `day` or `night` from its ⓘ pane, `room` on Today); Today's white panes are that theme, not selectors of Today's own; the older ink names are re-resolved inside a themed surface (why release notes were dark over Today); a terminal reads its colours again when the look changes (why it stayed white). *The browser*: the Browser tool is Chrome or Edge on the server's computer, driven by the server over the DevTools protocol on the process's own pipes and sent to the pane as pictures, with tabs, the address, back/forward/reload, pointer, keys, paste, dialogs; five agent tools (`browser_read`, `_navigate`, `_click`, `_type`, `_tabs`) that work only while a person has it open and has let their own agents in. *The terminal*: what a command prints loses its blank padding and one empty line stands before each prompt; typing before the first prompt is held and run at it. *Changes*: a release page (cover, description, a bar of what the release is made of, a tile per part with an icon, the notes under them), commits by day. *Apps as tabs*: a space of kind `app`; T3's pane has ⤢, and any app or page card has *Open as a tab*; T3's frame follows the look in the pane and is its own in the tab. *The grid*: the wheel zooms (a trackpad pans; a setting has the old way); the orb is right of you; a boundary's grab area is one section's edge; a card's right-click menu (frame, full screen, minimize, pin or unpin, fill or show as a card, float or dock, inputs and outputs in the right pane, open as a tab, copy a link, remove); a table can be pinned to its section and is drawn on the section's paper at its exact width; a section a person adds starts with one.

Not built, or not as asked: **only tables pin** (a note, a map or a picture spanning its section is not built; an app filling its section is the older *hosted app*, now also in the card's menu); **"create collections" from the menu was not built** (the collection card of §0.8 is unchanged); **T3's own page is not restyled**: it is T3's web client in a frame, and Foundation can only tell it light or dark; whether T3 follows that was not seen (T3 was not started); **minimize is not offered for images and videos** (they have no bar); **the browser is not a window on this computer**: it runs where the server runs, which on the desktop app is this computer, and on a hosted Foundation is the host; **Rune draws none of the new card states**.

**What shows it's right.** `apps/client/e2e/look.spec.ts` (9, Chromium on ports above 15000): Today's theme and the release page's ink there and on a grid; the terminal's ink on both without a restart, and its spacing; the wheel, Shift+wheel and a trackpad gesture; the orb; a new section's pinned table measured against its section; the card menu's minimize, restore, unpin, pin and remove; every grab area matching a section's edge; T3 as a tab with its row and mark; a grid pinned to night and the next back on paper. `apps/server/test/hostbrowser.test.ts` (6, one against the real Chrome on this machine: a page seen, read by ref, a link followed, typed into, a dialog answered, the process gone), `termtidy.test.ts` (6), `hostterm.test.ts` (3); `apps/client/test/release-outline.test.ts` (4), `sections.test.ts` (+2, the grab areas), `design.test.ts` (the token ratchet). Seen in a browser on a fresh copy, day and night: github.com/login (which refuses framing) in the tools pane. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-18_themes/` (12 shots and `NOTES.md`).

### 0.10 HyperForm: the company's apps, linked on Today and opened in Foundation (Josh, 2026-11-19)

**The ask.** "We need to start working on integrating with HyperForm which has all of the vibecoded projects hosted on them. For now we should just login to the website and pull out some of the links there." Today's right side with the right links: HyperForm (`dock.apps.normallab.co`), PartForm ("all of our parts, also possibly known as BOMForm", `partform.apps.normallab.co`), DeployForm ("keep track of customers", `deployform.apps.normallab.co`) and FactoryForm (`factory-form-factoryform.apps.normallab.co`; the message's `normalla.co` does not resolve). "We should also be able to open these up as WebPages in Foundation to take over a section, and have our agents be able to interact with them. They should be thoughtfully rendered, saving previews of them to be able to see them at a distance, but running the live versions of them as we get closer."

**What was found.** HyperForm is a deploy platform (git push → `https://<name>.apps.normallab.co`), with its own sign-in (a work Google account, `@normallab.co` or `@formindustries.co`) that every app it hosts shares, an MCP server at `/mcp`, a CLI, and `llms.txt`. Read without signing in: the dock answers with `X-Frame-Options: DENY`; PartForm redirects to its `/login`; DeployForm and FactoryForm answer 401 "requires a hyperform sign-in". So **a frame can't show any of them**: the sign-in refuses framing, and its cookie (SameSite=Lax) doesn't travel into a frame on another site. They need a real browser.

**What was built.**
- *The list* (`packages/protocol/src/apps.ts`): HyperForm, PartForm, DeployForm and FactoryForm lead it, each with the address it is hosted at (`url`) and `signIn: "hyperform"`; an environment variable still wins (`PARTFORM_URL`, then the older `BOMFORM_URL`). PartForm replaces the BomForm row and keeps BomForm's connector dot. FactoryForm's row is the hosted app, no longer the local API's address. The Chrome extension's fallback shelf gets the same addresses. The Shiba's digest names each address.
- *Today*: a linked app's row opens it in your own browser, as before; **⤢** beside it opens it in Foundation (`lib/openApp.ts`): a new section at the grid's right edge that the app fills (the section's host, named after it). Pressing again goes to the section it already has. An app with no address has no ⤢.
- *A page card* (`PanelDoc.via === "browser"`, `panels/HostPage.tsx`): the card is a tab of the built-in browser of 2026-11-18 (the real Chrome or Edge on the server's computer), not a frame. The browser's socket and pointer/key handling moved out of the Browser tool into a hook both use (`shell/hostBrowser.ts`). The server gives each card a tab of its own, kept while the browser runs (closed after 3 minutes with nobody looking); a card's socket can't turn to another card's tab, open tabs or close others'; a page a site opens from it (a sign-in window) is shown in the card and the card comes back when it closes.
- *By distance*: **near** (on screen, drawn at 30 % or more, the camera calm) the page is live, laid out at the card's own size whatever the zoom; **far** it is the picture the server last kept of it (`pagepictures.ts`: one JPEG per card under `data/pages/<board>/`, replaced at most every 4 s while someone looks, never while the tab is at another site than the card's own, so a sign-in provider's page is never kept), with its name and "seen 14:02"; the board carries `seenAt` so everyone's picture refreshes. Moving away pauses the pictures at once and closes the card's socket after 45 s. **In use** (⌘/Ctrl-click, as any card): back, forward, reload, the address, home, ↗ and Agents.
- *Nothing starts without a person*: the browser starts on a press ("Go live" on the card, ⤢ on Today, or the Browser tool's Start). That press holds for the visit (session storage), so the next page you come near goes live by itself. It is an admin's, as the Browser tool is; everyone else sees the kept picture and "Open in your browser ↗".
- *Agents*: the five `browser_` tools of 2026-11-18 reach a card's page: with **Agents** on (on the card or in the Browser tool; the person's own agents only), `browser_tabs` lists each card's tab with its `card` id, and an agent selects it, reads it and types into it. What it does shows on the page.
- *Any page card* can be moved between a frame and the real browser from its right-click menu ("Show it in the real browser" / "Show it in a frame").

**The dashboard, read once Josh had signed in (2026-11-19, in Claude's own browser pane, not Foundation's).** Ten projects: deployform, partform, factory-form, formindustries-metalform, gnatt-chart (Form Gantt), form-chess, airform (`airform.space`, the product site, public), form-figma (`form-figma-files…`: a storage service, not a page), flowform (`server.formresearch.ai`, stopped) and git (empty). Added to the list: **MetalForm, Form Gantt, Form Chess** (behind HyperForm's sign-in: each redirects to the dock's gate) and **Airform** (public, a frame). What the pages are: **PartForm is "Airform BOM"**, so it is BomForm, and behind HyperForm's sign-in it asks for a second one, OnShape's, by invitation; **DeployForm is an "Inventory & Install Tracker"** (units in inventory and installs by project, not a customer list), and its description says so now; FactoryForm opened on its factory twin.

**Not built, or not as asked.** **The list is still kept by hand**: it was read off the dashboard once; a new project on HyperForm does not appear by itself (plan: *hyperform-projects*). **Nobody has signed in inside Foundation's own browser**, so the real apps have not been seen in a card. **Google's sign-in was not tried in that browser** (§7). **HyperForm's MCP server, CLI and API are not wired**: listing its projects on Today, deploy state, and a machine certificate so the server reads an app without a person's session are the next slice (plan: *hyperform-projects*). **The live page is an admin's only** and runs where the server runs. **A picture is only as fresh as the last time an admin was near the page**: nothing refreshes it in the background (that would be a browser starting without a person). **Rune draws none of this.** **The page is sharp at up to 100 % zoom on a 1× screen**: zoomed in past the card's own size it is enlarged, not re-rendered.

**What shows it's right.** `apps/server/test/pagecard.test.ts` (4: the list and its addresses; a card's ask and its one-time key; the picture store refusing what isn't a JPEG and any path out of its folder; and against the real Chrome on this machine: two cards, two tabs, each socket kept to its own, the picture kept and told, not kept at another site, an agent reading a card's page with leave and refused without). `apps/client/e2e/pages.spec.ts` (5, Chromium driving the app while the server drives Chrome; DeployForm is a stand-in page the checks serve, the real one is never opened): Today's four rows and addresses; ⤢ making a section the page fills, live with no frame, at the card's size; a plain click not using it and Ctrl-click showing its bar; an agent refused, then with Agents on finding the card's tab, reading "Three customers are being deployed" and typing into its field; ⤢ again making no second card; zoomed out, the picture and "seen …" with the canvas hidden, and live again near with no press; a member seeing the picture and ↗, refused the browser (403); the rows on `#/design`. `today.spec.ts`, `look.spec.ts` and `card-use.spec.ts` still pass beside it; `npm test` (734, none failing) and typecheck are green. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-19_hyperform/` (6 shots and `NOTES.md`).

**PartForm in the built-in browser, and a page card at rest (2026-11-24, Josh: PartForm on the Form Industries grid was turned away by OnShape's sign-in; "I don't want it to take over scroll and zoom interactions, or navigation key strokes to move between panels, but clicking on buttons, or using ctrl + scroll wheel zoom should work").**
- *What was wrong.* That card was made by pasting PartForm's address, so it was a frame (no `via`), not a page of the built-in browser. PartForm's "Sign in with OnShape" goes to `oauth.onshape.com`, which answers `Content-Security-Policy: frame-ancestors 'self' *.onshape.com …`: OnShape's sign-in never loads in a frame, in any browser (WebView2 included). The built-in browser (headless Chrome on this computer) reaches it: "Sign in with OnShape" landed on OnShape's own sign-in page at the top level (checked 2026-11-24 with a scratch profile, nobody signed in). Its `navigator.userAgentData` brands read "Google Chrome", and the user agent's "HeadlessChrome" was already replaced.
- *Which cards are the browser's* (`pageInBrowser`, `packages/protocol/src/apps.ts`): what the card says wins (`via: "browser"`, or the new `via: "frame"`, set by the card menu's *Show it in a frame*). With nothing said, a page on HyperForm (`https://*.apps.normallab.co`) is the browser's, so the PartForm card already on the grid becomes one, with nothing rewritten. A pasted address whose site refuses a frame becomes a browser card, not a link out (`openUrlAsPanel`).
- *A live page card at rest* (`panels/HostPage.tsx`): a plain left click is the page's (its press, its drag, and the hover), so buttons and links work without ⌘/Ctrl-click. The wheel, ⌘/Ctrl-wheel (the grid zooms), the middle and right buttons (pan, the card's menu) and every key stay the grid's. After a click the server says whether the page's caret is in a field (`{t:"focus", editing}`, `hostbrowser.ts`); if it is, the card holds the keys so you can type ("Typing goes to this page · Esc Esc to leave"), until Esc twice, a click elsewhere, or a click in the page off a field. ⌘/Ctrl-click still puts it in use (the bar, and the wheel goes to the page). The "to use" hint shows only while the modifier is held over the card.
- *Not built.* **Frames** (any other site) are as they were: a frame can't take clicks without also taking the wheel, so they still need ⌘/Ctrl-click. The live page is still an admin's. **Nobody has signed in to OnShape (or HyperForm) in the built-in browser yet** (plan: *hyperform-sign-in*). Josh's desktop app gets this once its server on :4410 restarts and its client is updated.
- *What shows it's right.* `pagecard.test.ts` (the rule: HyperForm, a card's own say, machines' pages and other kinds); `e2e/pages.spec.ts`: a plain click presses the stand-in's button ("1 marked"), `x` and ↓ at rest never reach it, ⌘/Ctrl-wheel over it zooms the grid by more than 3 %, a plain wheel never reaches the page, a click in its field shows the typing note and "Hilo" lands there, Esc Esc gives the keys back, ⌘/Ctrl-click shows the bar; `card-use.spec.ts` unchanged and passing. Run in a clean worktree of this change alone, because the shared checkout held another session's unfinished files. `npm test` and typecheck green there. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-24_page-clicks/`.

### 0.11 ChatForm: the company's conversations (Josh, 2026-11-18)

**The ask.** Rebuild the Slack and communication framework the company uses, with Google Workspace mail, WhatsApp, Instagram and WeChat, as a top-level function beside Today that opens conversations between people and agents, T3 threads and agent sessions among them. Take what we use of Slack, with Mattermost as the model, and bring our Slack history across. Images, video, GIFs, notes, links, direct messages. Agents that chat and are plainly agents, wearing the avatars. A robust huddle system with video, taken into the grid, recorded and replayed with the work on the grid and CAD files; a small time primitive that rewinds a section.

**What a professional adds.** One conversation model: a channel, a DM, a group, a talk with an agent and a WhatsApp chat are all a channel, so threads, reactions, files, search, unread, huddles and export are written once. The server says who spoke; no request names its author, so an agent can never pass for a person. Nothing leaves the building without a person: an agent's reply to an outside conversation is a draft. History comes with us and can leave again. Bridges on the official routes only, each honest about what it lacks. A huddle is one room wherever it is shown, so taking it to a grid drops nobody. The build document, with the contract and who built what, is `docs/chatform.md`; the types are `packages/protocol/src/chat.ts`.

**Rawest material.** Mattermost's model and wire (`server/public/model/channel_member.go`, `websocket_message.go`: channel kinds, a per-channel count and a per-member read mark, root-id threads, pending ids, the event envelope). Slack's export format and help pages, and `mmetl`'s README. Foundation's own huddles, crew, agent runtime, T3 host and characters. The Gmail, WhatsApp Cloud, Instagram Messaging and WeCom references, from memory and unverified against the live services (§7).

**How it was built.** A lead session wrote the contract and the architecture, filed nine cards, and briefed seven builders (Opus) with files of their own and named seams: the core server and the place first, side by side; then history, bridges, agents and huddles together; then replay. Each was reviewed, its screens looked at, its suites run with the others', and committed by the lead.

**What was built, and what was not.** Built and checked: the core (channels, DMs, groups, threads, reactions, pins, edits, removals, files, link previews, search with `from:` `in:` `before:` `after:` `has:`, unread and mentions, a `/chat` socket, export); the place (`#/chat`: the pill beside Today with its badge, Chat · Agents · Inbox · Huddles, the sidebar, timeline, thread pane, composer, quick switcher); Slack's history in and ChatForm's out; four bridges and their drafts; agents that answer a mention in the thread as themselves, with five chat tools; huddles in channels with screen share, don't-record-me, seated agents, and taking a call to a grid. **Not built, or not verified** (said here rather than implied): no bridge has met its real service, and none can reach a customer on personal WeChat; the importer has not met a real Slack export; a T3 follow-up turn and a T3 agent answering in chat ran against the stand-in only; huddles were checked with two people on a drawn camera, never a real one; scheduled send, reminders, custom emoji, user groups, channel sections of your own, "also send to channel", saved items and notification sounds are not built; ChatForm is the web renderer's (nothing in Rune); mobile is untried. Replay and the clock were built last and checked in one scripted scenario only (a STEP and an STL in a channel, a huddle taken to a grid and recorded with an agent seated, replayed, the clock rewinding its section for one viewer): the real Vision Archive was down, so *From Vision* met a stand-in; a real model has not answered aloud in a huddle; a long session, the 45-minute cap and a big board are untried.

**What shows it's right.** `apps/server/test/chat.test.ts` (17), `chatimport.test.ts` (10), `chatbridges.test.ts` (17), `chatagents.test.ts` (11), `chathuddle.test.ts` (9) and `huddle.test.ts` (8) in a server suite with none failing; `apps/client/test/chat-*.test.ts` (24) in the client suite; `e2e/chatform.spec.ts` (three people: live delivery, the badge, a thread, a reaction, an edit, an upload, a DM hidden from the third, search, the switcher), `chatform-agents.spec.ts`, `chatform-huddle.spec.ts` (video both ways measured, the same peer connected through the move to a grid, a private huddle locked for a non-member), `huddle.spec.ts` unchanged. One real Claude turn on the subscription answered in its thread and remembered the question. A signed WhatsApp webhook ran end to end against the stand-in: an agent's draft was refused until a person sent it. The import CLI filled a temp data dir that a member and a non-member then read differently. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-18_chatform/`.

### 0.12 Foundation's avatars: the uploader (Josh, 2026-11-17)

**The ask.** "Visualize that the Characters are being exported to the Section on the left through the pipes, in that left section is a Node that can accept Character Input Cards through the flow interface, that shows that they have been set as avatars for Foundation to use anywhere. Breaking a connection will remove that, the characters can be individually connected to that uploader, or a section that holds many of them can be connected to that uploader. The uploader can force a manual upload, but also can set a timer. This should be something that can be hosted on the foundation server to run periodically." (After: "The Characters for the Agents should be uploaded to the main build of foundation to be used by other foundation clients.")

**What was built, and what was not.** The Character studio gets a column on its left, **Foundation avatars**, hosting an **uploader** card (`panel: "uploader"`, ⇪; server `avatars.ts`, protocol `avatars.ts`, client `UploaderView.tsx`); every studio gets it at the server's next start (`studioLayers`, run by `upgradeStudio`), with a wire from each `Characters · <Set>` section. **A section can be a wire's source** (`from.panel` a section id, port `characters`): it leaves by the section's edge facing its other end, as a hosted app's does, and brings every character card in the section (nested sections too). **Every character card has an output** (`character`, type `character`, violet `viz-8`); older cards get one when their board opens. Collections and an Output node wired in bring their characters. Character wires always flow (the pulse runs toward the uploader). **Wire in** on the card draws a wire backwards out of its input: let go on a card, or over a section (which rings violet). Wired, finished and approved is an **avatar**; wired but pending or unfinished **waits** (Approve on the tile); a wire taken off **archives** the avatars only it brought, wired back they're approved again; the first reading when the uploader is laid changes nobody's approval. **What an upload would take out of main shows before anyone presses**: every character main has that isn't an avatar here is a tile under *Leaving* (greyed), with why (not wired in, not an avatar here, not on this Foundation at all), and the tally counts them. **Upload now** (a person's press) and a **timer** (Off, hourly, 6 h, daily, weekly; a person's; checked every minute by the server, the next run moved on before it starts) write the avatars into `apps/server/starter/characters` in a worktree of `origin/main`, keep each unchanged file as it is (the manifest now records each file's archive sha256), commit only a change and push it, once more from a fresh main if main moved; nothing wired is refused (it would empty every install's set). Only a server started from a checkout can upload; the desktop's bundled server says so on the card. **Installs follow main**: a starter character main no longer lists is archived there at boot (kept, with its files), one main lists again is approved again. **Not built**: Rune doesn't draw the uploader or a section's wire; the upload copies pictures as the archive has them (PNG, not re-encoded as JPEG as `characters-export.mjs` does, so a new character adds a few MB to the repo); a character re-uploaded with new files still doesn't replace an install's existing copy (§7); no agent tool presses Upload or sets the timer (on purpose: it publishes a release to every install). **Not run**: an upload to the real `joshleong/Foundation` main (only against a throwaway bare repository); the live studio on `:4410` gets it when that server restarts. **Before the first real upload, a person's decision**: on a copy of this computer's studio the uploader reads *12 avatars · 10 leaving*: the cards of Ember, Shade, Scout and Yuki in 3D and of Kumo, Taro, Shiro, Kuro and the mesh Shade and Scout are off the grid (retired; their records and files kept), while main's set (from 2026-11-16) has all 22. An upload from there takes those ten out of main, and every install archives them at its next start. To keep them, put their cards back (or wire them in) before uploading.

**What shows it's right.** `apps/server/test/avatars.test.ts` (7): the studio's left column and its three wires, laid once; a section and a card wired in, avatar and waiting; a wire off archives, back approves, a person's archive isn't undone; an upload commits only a change (+/-, files byte for byte, untouched characters' files unchanged), then *same*, then a removal; nothing wired, one under way and a non-checkout refused; a timer due runs and moves on; an install withdraws and brings back. `characters.test.ts`, `studio-pipeline.test.ts` (an older studio's character cards gain only their port). `apps/client/e2e/avatars.spec.ts` (5, with the real starter set): the column, the uploader and the three drawn wires; 22 tiles and a greyed Upload now that says why; a wire deleted archives Anime's five and Wire in over the section brings them back; a card's output dragged onto the uploader wires one character; day, night and `#/design`. `council.spec.ts` lists the new section. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-17_avatars-uploader/`.

### 0.13 Progressive cached images & hosted ChatForm readiness (Josh, 2026-11-21)

**The ask.** Progressively load images, keep cached images warm in the interface while maintaining high performance, and push online hosting of Foundation's chat server forward.

**What a professional adds.** Move the image pyramid to the server first (`imagelod.ts` with pure-JS `jpeg-js` + `fast-png` so single-file desktop bundling stays intact): fast header-only probing (`probeImage`), decompression-bomb guard (64 MP / 16,384 px ceiling checked before decode), quantized width tiers (`64, 256, 512, 1024, 2048` px) to block cache-busting DoS, single-flight deduplication, single-decode `512 → 64` upload pre-warming, O(1)-budgeted disk LRU cache outside `data/files/` (excluded from backups), `ETag` + fast-path `304 Not Modified`, and `width`/`height` persisted on `files` and `ChatFile` for zero layout shift. Unify client caching (`imageCache.ts`) across **both** cookie and cross-origin bearer transports (L1 ref-counted RAM LRU + L2 bounded persistent Cache Storage `foundation-image-cache-v2`, wiped on `logout()`), drive network fetches through a viewport-aware priority scheduler with `AbortController` pan-away cancellation, reveal cold loads from a ~1.4 KB `64w` micro-preview into off-main-thread `img.decode()` opacity swaps, and shed off-screen grid GPU textures down to a tiny cached tier after 2.5 s. On the hosted server (`hosted.ts`), enforce an explicit hosted capability boundary that disables local-computer hardware tools (`terminal`, `browser`, `machines`, `vms`, `t3`, `vision`, `places`, `localDev`) with 403 + honest UI notices, add Admin People & Role management (`PeopleSection`, last-admin guard, audit log), add verified restore (`scripts/hosted-restore.mjs`) and preflight (`scripts/hosted-preflight.mjs`), and harden Caddy headers + Compose log rotation.

**What shows it's right.** `apps/server/test/imagelod.test.ts` (8/8), `apps/server/test/hosted-features.test.ts` (5/5), `apps/client/test/imageCache.test.ts` (9/9), `apps/client/test/design.test.ts` (4/4), `node scripts/desktop-server.mjs --check`, `npm run check:security`. Real Chromium E2E (`e2e/progressive-images.spec.ts` 4/4, `e2e/hosted-chat.spec.ts` 4/4): **98.4% cold-load byte reduction** (`2,652.4 KB` originals → `43.1 KB` via `?w=64`/`256`, `0` original downloads), **0 network requests** on L1 remount and on full `page.reload()` in both cookie and cross-origin desktop bearer modes, Cache Storage wipe on logout, full hosted ChatForm flow (#general, DM, thread, reaction, cached image, unread badges, offline drop & catch-up), hosted 403 tool boundaries with 0 auto-POSTs, Admin People role promotion persisted across reload, and `#/design` day & night specimens. Evidence: `docs/evidence/2026-11-21_images-hosted-chat/`.

### 0.14 ChatForm, second round: chats in the side menu, figures on a line, an Inbox that connects, Slack in step, and which server (Josh, 2026-11-20)

**The ask.** Channels dragged out of ChatForm into the left pane, with a category for chats in the selector; a chat opened there takes the whole area and folds ChatForm's own list. Avatars not in a circle: standing on a line that crops them. An Inbox that says what is connected and has buttons that start connecting. "It's unclear to what server ChatForm is being connected to, I assume we pulled in a side loading MatterMost server written in GO": a local server now, one on the rack later, and this one able to serve other clients on the network. A small pop-up that leads to a step-by-step guide to import Slack's channels or bridge them both ways.

**What was found.** There is no Mattermost server and never was: Mattermost was the model. ChatForm is Foundation's own server (`apps/server`), the one the desktop app starts on `localhost:4410`, with conversations in the same SQLite file as the grids. What was missing was the app saying so, and that server answering anyone but this computer.

**What was built.** *Side menu*: a **Chats** segment; a conversation pulled out of ChatForm's list is a slip in the hand that the side menu takes; kept chats are ordered rows with live unread, kept per person on the server (`person_prefs`, `chat.pinned`); opened from there, a chat is full-width with ChatForm's list folded. *Figures*: an agent is drawn chest-up behind a baseline at every size, never a disc. *Inbox*: **This Foundation**, then a card per service with its state in a line and one forward button (Connect your Gmail; a step wizard for WhatsApp, Instagram and WeChat with console links, copy rows, sealed fields and a Check that asks the provider); pasted keys are sealed in `chat_bridge_secrets` and never come back. *Slack*: a live bridge per linked channel (`bridges/slack.ts`): Slack's messages, replies, edits, reactions and files arrive here as their authors, a person's message here goes to Slack as *Name via Foundation*, an agent's waits as a draft, nothing loops; a nudge and a guide with two paths, *Move in* and *Keep both in step*. *The server*: the panel says which server it is and what it serves; **On your network** (off by default; an admin's press at the server's computer) adds a listener on this computer's private addresses, serves the built page there, and signs a teammate in by a one-time invitation; what runs code, spends, or holds credentials answers only this computer. `docs/server.md` is the recipe for the rack.

**Not built, or not verified.** Nothing has met the real Slack, Google, Meta or WeCom; the manifest changes are in the repo and not applied to the real app. No second computer, phone or firewall prompt was involved: "another computer" in the checks was this one by its LAN address. A browser at a LAN `http` address can't send a camera or microphone (not a secure page); the installed app pointed at the address should, by Chromium's rule for `localhost`, and that was not run in the installed app. The built-in server the desktop app carries has no page for teammates (a checkout's `npm run build` does). Google sign-in can't finish from another computer. WhatsApp, Instagram and WeChat can't call a server that has no public address; the wizard says so. Outbound reactions to Slack, deletions inside Slack threads, a landing line while carrying a chat in, touch, a "…" on ChatForm's rows.
