- **Lifting a docked card reflows the cards after it** (they close the gap at once), and those cards render again because they really move. The whole-board re-render on press is fixed (2026-11-07). Holding the gap until the drop would move the remaining cost to the drop; that's a design call.
- ~~**Other whole-store subscribers**~~: fixed 2026-11-08. `GridViewport`, `WireLayer`, `PortWires`, `MapView` and `Present` read slices (`state/slices.ts`), and local awareness no longer notifies (§5). Still on every remote awareness change (other people's cursors): `PeerCursors`, `CrewCursors`, `TopBar`, `Talk` and the side pane's activity, through `useBoardTick`. Those are cheap but unmeasured.
- **A live page that has loaded stays mounted** when it scrolls off or the zoom drops (`LiveFrame` keeps it, so it needn't reload). So the live-frame budget is really "≤ the frames that have been on screen" (8 after the perf run's pan). Unloading after a while off screen would trade a reload for memory; not decided.
- **Framing a section** (F, double-click) zooms to its cards, but since 2026-11-07 keeps the section's full width (so its top-right tools) in the frame whenever that still fits at the 105 % cap, or the section is less than 1.6× wider than what it holds. At 1440×900 on the company board, four framed sections' tools went from 1,561–1,841 px (off a 1,440 px screen) to 1,002–1,304. A very wide sparse section still frames its cards and leaves the tools to a pan.
- **A wire from a split app section can run tight along a neighbour's edge.**
- **find_images can't see inside images.** It matches titles, names, comments and where an image came from. A picture shared with no words around it is only found by browsing.
- **Laid-out sections are a default track wide.** A long document or a big grid is fitted (down to 40 %) and past that clipped with Expand, rather than scrolling inside its section; sizing the track to the layout would affect neighbours in that column.
- **Windows runs** (2026-11-07, on a Windows 11 machine): `npm test` (26 + 46), typecheck, `npm run desktop` and the packaged build (Desktop, §3). Found there: Node before 22.16 has no FTS5 in `node:sqlite`, so the server can't start (`engines` now says 22.16; the README did say 22.13); the Vision test's stand-in CLI was a `#!` file Windows can't start (now `t3.cjs`, and a `T3_CLI` that names a script runs under Node). Still open on Windows:
  - **Stopping the launcher from outside stops its children, after a second or two** (2026-11-07). On Windows, `scripts/dev.mjs` hands each child to a detached watchdog (`scripts/dev-watchdog.mjs`). When the launcher dies, however it dies, or npm above it in `npm run dev` does, the watchdog lists processes once through PowerShell and ends the children's whole trees; Kiri keeps running. `node scripts/dev-orphans-check.mjs` proves it on :15441/:15431 (before: 9 processes and both ports left; after: all gone in 4–7 s). macOS/Linux still rely on process groups, so a `kill -9` of the launcher there leaves them running, and `npm run desktop` hasn't been through the check. `FOUNDATION_CLIENT_PORT` moves Vite off 1430.
  - **Windows can reserve the dev ports.** Hyper-V and WSL reserve blocks of the dynamic port range at boot. That range is 49152+ by default, but on Josh's machine it's 1024–15000, so after a reboot :1430 or :4410 can fail to bind (WSAEACCES; `netsh int ipv4 show excludedportrange protocol=tcp` lists the blocks). The packaged app's :31440 sits above both ranges for this reason.
  - **A packaged app on a hosted server** (2026-11-08): its page is http://localhost:31440, so a server elsewhere is cross-site and the `SameSite=Lax` cookie doesn't travel; it now signs in for a bearer and uses socket tickets (above). Verified in a browser (page :16430, server 127.0.0.1:16410), not yet in the packaged exe. Machines (`requirePerson`) accept the app's bearer as a person; agent bearers still can't start them.
  - The REA job now starts on Windows without a shell (2026-11-07, `rea.ts`): npx runs as npm's `npx-cli.js` under Foundation's Node, and Claude Code as `claude.exe` (or the `cli.js` an npm `claude.cmd` wraps). The target must be an http(s) address or an existing path, never one starting with `-`; a refused job says why on its card; on Windows an app path with `% ! ^ & | < >` is refused, because npx hands rea-agents to cmd.exe. The resolved npx answered `--version` here, but no real REA job has run on Windows (or anywhere) yet. Vision Code hand-offs now find T3 on Windows and Linux (2026-11-07, `t3paths.ts`): on Josh's machine the CLI at `C:\Dev\Vision\.tools\code-server\t3.exe` and the store at `%APPDATA%\co.normallab.vision\code` were found and its threads read, but no real hand-off has run on Windows yet. The agent starts Claude Code without a shell on Windows too (2026-11-07, `agent.ts` via `rea.ts`): `claude.exe` runs as is and an npm-installed `claude.cmd` runs the `cli.js` it wraps under Foundation's Node; when it can't start, the subscription runtime shows as off with the reason (Settings › Services, and the run's error). Proven with a stand-in, not a real turn. A Windows CI job (`npm test`, typecheck and `cargo build` on windows-latest) would prove it on every push.
- **Hand-offs reach Vision Code through its CLI on the same machine.** A hosted Foundation can't run it; it needs a T3 pairing link and a stored client credential, which is a decision for Josh.
- **A hand-off thread's file writes wait for approval in Vision Code** (Supervised, by design). Nothing comes back until someone approves there.
- **Exports are found by watching a folder**, every 4 s. A thread that writes elsewhere brings nothing back; the brief tells it where.
- **Reviews return as asks to people**, not as a verdict from Vision's own review tools.
- **Today reads every board on each load** to find mentions, stages and changes. Fine at today's handful of boards; past a few dozen it wants an index of mentions and owners kept as boards change.
- **"Your work" matches are lexical** (the FTS index against the profile's words), so a stage named "05 · Review" on a rim-drive pipeline matches "rim drive" by its body. Semantic search (phase 4) would rank these better.
- **Mentions are `@name` only**: `@First`, `@Full Name`, `@email` or its local part. Two people sharing a first name are both "tagged". A real @-picker in notes (with ids) would fix it.
- **The Shiba is slow to answer** (~15–20 s): each turn starts a headless Claude Code run with Foundation's MCP. The API runtime would stream faster (needs credits).
- **The Shiba isn't one of the crew.** It talks to one person on Today, not on a board, so it has no cursor; if asked to change a board, its writes are signed "Foundation Agent" rather than a crew name, and it can't call in helpers.
- **The Shiba doesn't speak aloud.** Its words are type. The browser's speech synthesis (local on macOS) or Vision's `@pacific/voice` (paid, consented per talk, as in HomeForm) are the two ways to add a voice. Listening uses Web Speech, which in Chrome sends audio to Google (as the HUD mic does).
- **The extension's popup reads Today with Foundation's cookie** from the extension's origin: checked in Chromium (Playwright's, the same engine as Chrome) on 2026-11-11, signed in and out. Not yet in branded Chrome with a hosted Foundation on HTTPS, nor force-installed by policy (the admin's `foundationUrl` is checked only through a stand-in `chrome.storage.managed`).
- **The store images are made from the e2e's seeded day** (`STORE_ASSETS=1`), on localhost; the popup's footer is shown with the release address. Retake them when the listing changes.
- **The Web Store reviewers can't sign in** (company accounts only). `STORE.md` says so and offers a viewer account as an option; `#/privacy` must be reachable from the internet, or published on a public page.
- **The new tab shows Foundation's address in the omnibox** (it hands off to Foundation's own page so sign-in and the Shiba's WebGL run there). Chrome may ask once whether to keep the extension's new tab on a person's own install; it isn't expected to for one force-installed by policy (unchecked until the first rollout).
- **No profile form.** Title, team and the words for "your work" are set by telling the Shiba. A small form on Today would make the first run less dependent on the agent.
- **Kiri's Windows installer is unsigned** (Kiri's Authenticode signing isn't in place yet; see its RELEASES.md). `npm run kiri:install` checks the MSI against a SHA-256 pinned in `scripts/kiri.mjs`, copied from that release's `SHA256SUMS`. That catches a corrupted or swapped download, but not a bad release published as such: the pin and the file come from the same GitHub release.
- **Foundation installs Kiri without Kiri's first-run window.** It imports Kiri Linux into WSL as `kiri` and starts `kiri-win.exe --distro kiri`: the same steps as choosing "Kiri Linux" there. But it doesn't add the `kiri` docker context, Kiri's `bin` on PATH, or start-at-sign-in. Opening Kiri from the Start menu shows its setup once (choose Kiri Linux; it finds the distro). Removing it is Apps & features → Kiri, which also unregisters the distro and deletes its disk.
- **Kiri's engine in WSL outlives its app.** If `kiri-win` quits, the machines keep running inside WSL but Foundation can't reach them: the card says Out of reach until Start (or `npm run kiri`) brings the app back. Kiri also keeps running after `npm run dev` stops, as Docker does.
- **`scripts/kiri.mjs` has been run on Windows only.** Its macOS (`.dmg`, `open -a Kiri`) and Linux (`.deb`/`.rpm`, `kiri daemon`) paths are written from Kiri's docs and not yet tried.
- **Machines run on the computer the server runs on.** A hosted Foundation would start machines on the host, which is a different trust question: who may start what, and on whose hardware. Ports are on that computer's 127.0.0.1, so a page opened from a port works only in a browser on the same computer (the desktop app, `npm run dev`).
- **A machine's terminal is one shell for everyone, and anyone who can edit the board can type into it as root.** Board roles are the only gate; there are no per-machine permissions. The shell lasts 5 minutes after the last person leaves, and is lost when the server restarts (the machine keeps running; the card reconnects to a new shell). Its screen is held in memory only, not archived.
- **Agents' commands wait in the server's memory** (2026-11-08). A restart ends every wait (the agent's call fails with it) and the card's waiting asks say withdrawn when the board loads again. A command runs as the machine's root user without a terminal, so anything that prompts fails; `timeout` stops it where the image has one (busybox and coreutils do), and the server cuts the stream 5 s later either way. The engine can't kill an exec, so a command still running past its limit in an image without `timeout` keeps running in the machine. ~~People with edit rights could write a fake ask into `machineAsks` through their sync socket~~: fixed the same night, a change from a sync socket to a map only the server writes (`machineAsks`, and `huddles`, which had the same gap) is put back the way the server left it (`guardServerMaps` in `boards.ts`, `test/server-maps.test.ts`). The first command after a machine started took 6.2 s on Docker Desktop here; later ones 0.2–0.6 s. No real model has used `machine_run` or `crew_work` yet.
- **The terminal at zooms other than 100 %**: since 2026-11-09 (#53) mouse events bound for the terminal are re-sent with the pointer's place in xterm's own pixels (`panels/scaledMouse.ts`), so a selection starts under the pointer at any zoom or fit. Checked with a real xterm drawn at 50 % and 150 % (it selected the letters dragged over, where it had selected others before); not yet with a running machine on the board.
- **A port opened as a page follows its machine** (2026-11-07): the page knows its card and port (`fromMachine`). It's live while the machine runs, at whatever host port the engine gave it, and otherwise shows the machine's state in place of the frame: Stopped with **Start it** (a person) and Show the machine, Starting, Error, or gone; it reloads by itself when the port answers again. While on screen it asks the server to re-read the engine every 5 s, so a machine stopped outside Foundation shows there too. Checked against Docker Desktop (`docs/evidence/2026-11-07_machine-port-stale/`). Not yet: a viewer in a real browser, Kiri, and a server inside the machine slower than the 20 s wait; a machine on a sleeping Kiri reads Stopped on its page, not Out of reach. Seen meanwhile: a card's hover bar covers its port pills, and a page in a section fitted to about 27 % stays a poster (not reproduced. Since 2026-11-08 the gate is zoom × fit, so such a page is a poster below zoom 1.1 by design. Under the old rule it would have run at any zoom ≥ 0.3, so the board was probably zoomed out further than that).
- **The crew has had three real model turns, all typed into the sheet, on one machine** (2026-11-07, Claude Code subscription, `docs/evidence/2026-11-07_crew/` 10–14). Each read the board first, made its things in one new section, called `bring_view` once at the end, and was honest about offline data ("not filled in yet: FactoryForm is offline"). Twice it called in a helper for the separate part (a standup checklist), and the helper signed its note. 29–44 s from ask to done. The automated two-person e2e still runs on the `script` runtime: a real turn isn't repeatable and spends the plan. Not yet seen from a real model: a voice ask, an ask while someone else is watching, an ask that should *not* bring you anywhere.
- **Kimi and DeepSeek run through T3's Claude agent, and nothing about them is proven yet** (2026-11-12). T3 0.0.44's drivers are codex, claudeAgent, cursor, grok, opencode and antigravity (`builtInDrivers.ts`); there's no Kimi, DeepSeek or OpenAI-compatible one, so Foundation adds each as a Claude-driven instance with `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` and `ANTHROPIC_MODEL` set (T3's own docs show this for a router). The defaults are from the vendors' pages and third-party guides read on 2026-11-12, not from a run: Moonshot `https://api.moonshot.ai/anthropic` with `kimi-k3` (guides disagree: some say `kimi-k3[1m]`), DeepSeek `https://api.deepseek.com/anthropic` with `deepseek-v4-pro`; the form lets a person change both. Local is Ollama's Anthropic-compatible API on `http://127.0.0.1:11434` (Ollama 0.14 or newer), any model pulled there; this PC has no model server, and Kimi's open weights are far too large for it. T3 lists an added agent as ready as soon as Claude Code is installed: a wrong key or model shows only when a thread fails. OpenAI is Codex: on this PC the `codex` program isn't installed.
- **An agent asked through T3: what's still rough** (2026-11-13). Its pass to the board works up to the model (SPEC §3), but no real agent has used it. Each ask is its own T3 project (its folder holds its pass), so T3's project list grows by one per ask. The launch line names a tool file by a relative path: a thread started from T3's own page in a folder that has no such file will fail to start on those agents, so start threads from Foundation (T3's page is for approvals and transcripts). `--strict-mcp-config` keeps this computer's other Claude connectors away from board agents; T3's own tool server for a thread, if it gives one, may be shut out by it too (untried). A supervised thread's other approvals (files, commands) are answered in T3's page, not on the board; a thread waiting there says so on its face. The link Open makes signs that browser in to T3, which listens on this computer's 127.0.0.1 only. Presence is kept in the server's memory: after a restart of Foundation's server a running thread goes on in T3, its face is gone, and its pass lasts until its 12 hours run out.
- **Foundation's T3 and Vision Code's are two instances** (2026-11-12): separate homes, ports and threads. Foundation's crew (its own `claude -p` runs) doesn't go through T3 yet, so the board's agents and this computer's agents are two lists in one pane. T3 stops when Foundation's server stops (a hard kill of the server on Windows can leave `t3.exe` running; the next Start takes the next free port). The pane polls every 3 s while it's open. T3's Claude uses this computer's `~/.claude` sign-in unless an instance is given its own folder.
- **The agent group is who's aiming where, now** (2026-11-12). An agent is in a section only while its cursor aims there, and it's gone from every group 2.4 s after its run ends: nothing is kept between runs, so there are no agents that belong to a section, no idle ones, and no subscription named per agent yet (plan: *posted-agents*). An agent that hasn't aimed anywhere yet is in no group; the pane lists it under *Not in a section yet*. Agents that reach the board over MCP without a crew id have no face.
- **A section's tab that has come down with the view sits over what's under it** (2026-11-12): with a section zoomed past its top edge, the tab stands just under the top bar, over the section's own cards. It only shows on hover or when pinned. A pin is kept on this device, not shared, and a pin on a section drawn in miniature (inside a grid) doesn't show until that grid is opened.
- **The agents pane and search share the right side** (2026-11-12): opening one closes the other. Both at once was left out to keep the grid its paper on a laptop.
- **What a real turn found, fixed the same day.** (1) It hooked the connector up first, so the connector hosted the new section and the table and notes landed hidden under it. Agents' cards now un-host the app (above). (2) It wrote a markdown table in a note, which showed as raw pipes: notes render tables now. (3) Cursor labels read "wiring port_… → port_…" (the model wires by id): they name the cards now. (4) The helper's brief was logged as the person's ask; the lead now signs "called in Finch: …" instead. (5) "arranged 4 cards in sec_…": the section's name now.
- **Who did what, in activity** (fixed 2026-11-08): the HTTP tools door is the person only from their browser; a bearer token is "Name's agent". People's grid edits are logged from their sync socket, gathered until they pause (2.5 s, 15 s at most) in the server's memory, so a restart in that window drops those lines (the edits themselves are kept). Offline edits are logged when the device reconnects, at that time. Only sections and cards added, removed, renamed or moved count: a resize, typing, cells and an app's outputs don't. A card made through the HTTP door by a person still has no `createdBy` (by design: people's cards name nobody).
- **`wires.test.ts`'s "avoiding stays fast with a crowded board" is a timing test** (20 cold routes in under 400 ms). Under load (several sessions and agents on this machine) it failed once in each of two runs and passed on rerun.
- **Cards say which agent made them** (2026-11-07): `createdBy` is on every object now; notes and tables a crew agent writes show "✦ Name" in violet (a note at its bottom left, a table beside its name), panels keep the tool's actor. Cards made by `lay_out` and the pipeline's Vision briefs don't carry it yet, and external MCP agents (no crew id) don't either.
- **A crew cursor is drawn where this browser lays the target out.** Two people with different section scroll see it at different places in a section; a card scrolled out of view inside its section gets the cursor at the section's nearest edge.
- **Aim-then-act adds about half a second per write** (`FOUNDATION_CREW_TRAVEL_MS`, 520 ms; reads don't wait). A long build of many cards is slower than before by design. Set it to 0 for scripts.
- **Space now does two things**: held, it's still the pan with a drag; held still, it listens after 240 ms; tapped, it opens typing. Someone who holds Space and waits before dragging sees the sheet flash. Measure on a real trackpad with real people.
- **Agents asked from the sheet run without a tab.** Their sessions are listed in the side pane, the roster's Open shows them while they work, and since 2026-11-07 the open sheet lists your last three finished asks for 15 minutes with Open. The list is this tab's memory: a reload forgets it (the side pane still has the sessions).
- **Helpers share the asker's sign-in and run on the same runtime.** A helper on the subscription runtime is a second `claude -p` at once on the person's plan. On the API runtime it spends credits; the person's ask is what allows it (two at most).
- **The mic level opens the microphone a second time** (getUserMedia beside Web Speech, which can't share its stream). Since 2026-11-07 it does so only when the microphone is already allowed (Permissions API), so it never causes a second prompt; until then, and where the permission can't be read, the bars breathe. Headless Chromium: not yet allowed → 0 getUserMedia calls; allowed → 1, live bars. Not checked in the desktop app's WebView2.
- **The flowform importer's fixture rooms were never committed** (`apps/server/test/fixtures/flowform/data/rooms`): the root `.gitignore`'s `data/` caught them, so on a fresh clone `flowform-import.test.ts` crashed and `npm test` was red on `main`. Its six tests now skip and say why, and the rule is `/data/`. Whoever has the rooms should commit them. `main` also didn't typecheck: `index.ts` used `requireBoard`/`hiddenBoards`/`canSeeBoard` without importing them from `members.ts` (fixed).
- **The e2e client port :1450 sits in a block Windows reserves on this machine** (1437–1536). `E2E_CLIENT_PORT` moves it (`E2E_CLIENT_PORT=15450 npm run e2e -w @foundation/client`).
- **Cards at rest have no title** (by design); hosted apps and pipeline stages name themselves, and since 2026-11-07 so does a connector shown as a card (14/600 above its status pill). Other panel cards (links, files) still read by their body.

---

- **ChatForm, as built 2026-11-18** (§0.11; `docs/chatform.md` §6 has the server's exact behaviour):
  - **Nothing outside has been met.** The four bridges and the Slack importer ran against stand-ins and a fixture. Unknown until they meet the real thing: Google returning a refresh token, Meta's media hosts, WeCom's trusted-IP check, a multi-GB export's speed, a renamed channel's folder name. Customers on personal WeChat can't be reached (WeCom's app reaches the organisation only; Customer Contact, 微信客服 and an Official Account adapter are not built). Instagram sends text only and its token lapses after 60 days. Gmail's Pub/Sub push is written and untested
  - **Unread counts every message**: replies and system lines (a join, a pin) make a channel unread, unlike Slack. After history is merged into a live channel the count is kept exactly but the *New* rule sits by count, not on the exact message
  - **Admins can't moderate what they can't read**: a private channel or a DM they aren't in is closed to them, archive and removal included
  - **Imports**: an expired file link becomes a placeholder and is not fetched again by a later top-up; a thread broadcast shows only in its thread; one Slack workspace per ChatForm; a top-up looks back 14 days from the newest message here; custom emoji stay as `:name:`; canvases, lists and Slack Connect are not brought in. The CLI writes the same SQLite file as a running server: run it on a copy first
  - **Agents in chat**: turns run with the company board as their board context; *is typing* lingers up to 5 s after a reply; T3 host threads can't be read here (their store is under `<data>/t3`), so they say "Finished in T3"; caps are 2 turns at once and 8 a minute per person
  - **Huddles**: an agent's voice is each browser's `speechSynthesis` (it differs by machine); a shared screen is recorded as its own file by the sharer; opting out of recording is on the person's own browser, so a changed client could still record what it receives; a channel huddle's transcript is searchable in the Huddles tab only, not ⌘K; still a mesh (about six people)
  - **The place**: the members popover of an open channel shows a count, not a list; the pinned list comes from loaded messages; the Inbox refreshes every 20 s rather than on the socket's bridge events; a link preview's `og:image` is fetched once by Foundation's server (the preview guard, png/jpeg/gif/webp/avif, 5 MB) and kept in the archive, carried by its message, so viewers read `/files/…` (2026-11-26; one that can't be fetched is dropped). A separate Rust chat server still sends the remote address, which waits for a press
  - **Replay**: a take keeps one grid per recorded stretch and is kept for good (no retention screen; about 0.2 MB a minute estimated on a busy grid, never measured), stopping at 45 minutes or 48 MB with a line that says so; the clock rewinds one section, not the sections inside it; nobody's clocks are in step with anyone else's; OpenCascade still reads a STEP on the page's own thread (over 15 MB waits for a press); *From Vision* reads only a local Archive at `VISION_ARCHIVE_URL` in development mode, and Vision's cloud Archive can't be reached without a Vision sign-in; `chatform-huddle.spec.ts` once failed to connect its two browsers under load and passed alone
  - **Not built**: scheduled send, reminders, custom emoji, user groups, your own channel sections, saved items, "also send to channel", notification sounds and desktop notifications, slash commands and incoming webhooks; anything in Rune; a phone layout

- **ChatForm's second round, as built 2026-11-20** (§0.14; `docs/server.md`):
  - **On your network is plain http**: anyone who can watch the network can read it. Fine for trying it in the office; the rack server needs HTTPS in front. On this computer the Wi-Fi is marked *Public* and Windows already lets Node answer on Public networks (it was so before this work, and nothing was changed): with the switch on, anyone on that Wi-Fi reaches the sign-in page
  - **A browser at a LAN address has no camera or microphone** (not a secure page), so no huddles there; the installed app pointed at the address is the way, unchecked in the installed app itself
  - **The built-in server the app carries has no page for teammates**; a checkout's `npm run build` makes one. *Build the page* was never pressed in a check, and the switch surviving a restart is untested
  - **Google sign-in can't finish from another computer** until Flowform accepts the server's address (§6 #2); invitations are the way in, last 7 days as a session, and never make an admin
  - **Local-only may be too strict for teammates**: making an agent token, saving an app and allowing an app's tool answer only the server's computer
  - **Slack bridge**: deletions inside threads aren't seen and edits inside threads arrive with the thread's next reply; a send whose answer from Slack is lost can post twice on *Try again*; reactions made here don't go to Slack; removing a mirrored message here doesn't remove it in Slack; it polls every 10 s unless the Slack app's events are arriving
  - **Webhook services can't reach this computer**: WhatsApp, Instagram and WeChat call the server from outside, so they wait for the rack server or a tunnel
  - **Side menu**: no landing line while carrying a chat in from ChatForm; touch doesn't start a pull; ChatForm's rows have no "…" (right-click or Shift+F10)
  - **Figures**: a 3D character in the Agents head uses the portrait's framing and was not looked at

## 8. Plan

**The plan is a kanban board** (2026-11-07, Josh: "the plan forward should be a kanban board that we move through issues").

- **`docs/plan.json`** is the plan: every bug, plan and hookup as a card with a column, a milestone, an area and (once pushed) its GitHub issue.
- **GitHub issues** are where the team moves cards: `npm run plan:push` makes or updates one issue per card (labels `plan`, `kind: …`, `status: …`, `area: …`; a milestone each; Done closes it), and `npm run plan:pull` reads columns, titles and bodies back, and brings in any issue labelled `plan` that was opened on GitHub. `npm run plan:project` also lays them out as a GitHub Projects board (needs `gh auth refresh -s project`). `npm run plan:check` validates the file (also in `npm test`).
- **Settings → Plan** in the app shows the board, as a kanban card on a grid of Foundation's own (2026-11-15): the copy bundled with the build, refreshed from the server's file. An admin can drag a card to another column; the move writes the file and the card says *moved here* until it's pushed.

Columns: **Needs you** (a key, an install, a decision) · **Next** (in order) · **Doing** · **Review** (built; waiting to merge or for a person to check) · **Backlog** · **Done**.

Milestones (what the old phases became; finished phases 1 and 2 are gone from the plan):

| Milestone | What it covers |
|---|---|
| Polish | Bugs and rough edges in what ships (most of §7) |
| Vision loop | Hand-offs on Windows, an export seen from a real thread, Release → BomForm, a pairing link for a hosted server |
| The crew | A real model turn, crew-wide stop, follow and point, spatial voice |
| Company brain | BomForm diffed, the daily digest, spoken conversations |
| Search depth | Semantic search, Slack per person, Drive and Figma |
| Apps first-class | The registry, foundation.json, wires that call MCP, machines next |
| Huddles | A local transcriber, live Slack, an SFU (opt-out and screen share landed with ChatForm, 2026-11-18) |
| ChatForm | The real services connected, Slack's export brought in, replay and the clock, then what Slack has that we skipped (scheduled send, reminders, custom emoji, notifications) |
| Ship | CI on three systems, signed installers, a hosted server (the header-capable sync transport is in) |
| Today | Foundation for Chrome to everyone (hosted address, the Web Store item, the admin's force-install); Data Room goals; a profile form and the Shiba's first-run walkthrough; spoken replies; a mention index |

### Session routine (for every human or agent picking this up)

1. Read SPEC.md (this), DESIGN.md, and the last entries in `docs/evidence/`.
2. `npm install && npm test && npm run typecheck`. If red, fixing that is the session.
3. Take the top card in **Next** (Settings → Plan, or the issues labelled `status: next`), or a §7 issue if it's worse. Move it to Doing in `docs/plan.json`.
4. UI lands on `#/design` first, on tokens only. Check light and night, and 260/400/560-wide panels.
5. Capture evidence (screenshots and numbers) in `docs/evidence/<date>_<slice>/`.
6. Update §3 status and §7 issues honestly, and move the card (Review or Done). Commit with a message that says what changed and how it was checked; `npm run plan:push` when you have GitHub access.
